Yeah, I think the staying-still part is honestly what bothers me the most. Like, attention is a problem too, but the thing that made me come in was that feeling like I’m crawling out of my skin whenever I’m supposed to just sit there in the radiology suite or in class.

Yeah, I do. Even if it’s something I actually like, like editing photos or sketching, I still can’t keep it… quiet, I guess.

I’ll start tapping my pencil or humming without realizing it, or I’ll keep getting up to grab something, then sit back down, then get up again. Sometimes I’ll start talking to whoever’s nearby, even if they’re trying to focus. Like in the lab, or at home, people will be like, “Can you settle down?” and I’m not even trying to be annoying, I just feel like I have to do something.

Um… strong mood swings? I don’t know, I’m not sure I’d call them strong. Like, I can get irritated or worked up sometimes, but I feel like most people do, right?

It’s just… it gets really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience restlessness or fidgeting in quiet activities outside of work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if it’s something I actually like, like editing photos or sketching, I still can’t keep it… quiet, I guess.\n\nI’ll start tapping my pencil or humming without realizing it, or I’ll keep getting up to grab something, then sit back down, then get up again. Like, when I’m sitting still I definitely notice the sitting still part more than anything—like my leg bouncing or needing to shift around. Focus is… I don’t know, it depends what’s going on. Sometimes I’m listening, but then I’ll get caught thinking about, like, whether I parked in the right place or if I need to message someone back, and then I realize I missed part of what was said. But I don’t know if that’s a focus thing or just me being uncomfortable sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I do. Like at work, if a supervisor is explaining how they want a positioning step done, I’ll jump in before they’re even finished—like I’m trying to show I know it, or answer the question right away. And then they’ll finish and I realize I missed the one part that actually mattered.\n\nIt’s kind of embarrassing, because I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve really… waited, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsivity causing problems in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly with supervisors and the team, I guess. Like, it’s not just “oh, he talks too much” or whatever—my clinical evaluations have actually gotten worse. They said I was disrupting patient flow and making teamwork harder, which… yeah, that was pretty hard to hear.\n\nI’m on a formal remediation plan now, and I keep thinking about it even when I’m not there—like, I’ll be driving home and then remember some random thing I said too fast, or how I cut in when someone was trying to coordinate the next patient. Sorry, I’m kind of jumping around. But yeah, it’s causing real problems. I’m honestly worried if I don’t get it under control, I could lose my spot in the program.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they've finished speaking in social situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I notice it way more in the program because there are consequences right away.\n\nLike, if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me something, I’ll jump in with the answer before they’re done talking, almost like I’m trying to get ahead of it. And then they finish and I realize I missed the part that actually mattered. So I know I do it there. Socially, I guess I’d have to think about it more—I’m not as aware of it unless someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or restless when you're doing things like watching TV or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like even watching TV, I’m shifting around or getting up for no real reason, but it’s worse when I’m supposed to be sitting still in the radiology suite or in class. That’s actually a big part of why I came in—I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin when I’m expected to just sit there.\n\nI’ll tell myself, okay, just tough it out, don’t make it obvious, but then I’m pacing, or changing positions, or finding some excuse to go do something. It’s been going on for months, and I keep thinking I should be able to just stop doing it, but I haven’t really been able to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying still is more bothersome to you than your difficulty paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the staying-still part is honestly what bothers me the most. Like, attention is a problem too, but the thing that made me come in was that feeling like I’m crawling out of my skin whenever I’m supposed to just sit there in the radiology suite or in class.\n\nAnd it’s not like I haven’t tried to just deal with it. For months I’ve been telling myself, okay, just sit still, don’t make it obvious, but then I’m shifting around, or pacing, or finding some excuse to get up and move. It’s just… it gets really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience restlessness or fidgeting in quiet activities outside of work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if it’s something I actually like, like editing photos or sketching, I still can’t keep it… quiet, I guess.\n\nI’ll start tapping my pencil or humming without realizing it, or I’ll keep getting up to grab something, then sit back down, then get up again. Sometimes I’ll start talking to whoever’s nearby, even if they’re trying to focus. Like in the lab, or at home, people will be like, “Can you settle down?” and I’m not even trying to be annoying, I just feel like I have to do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience restlessness or fidgeting when you're relaxing with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, with friends or family too. Like if we’re just hanging out and it’s supposed to be relaxed, I’ll still start tapping on something or humming, or I’ll get up a bunch for no real reason.\n\nIt’s kind of the same as when I’m editing photos or sketching, which I actually like doing—I can’t keep it quiet for long. I’ll start talking to whoever’s nearby, even if they’re doing their own thing, and then people at home are like, “Can you just settle down?” I don’t mean to be distracting, it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: Um… strong mood swings? I don’t know, I’m not sure I’d call them strong. Like, I can get irritated or worked up sometimes, but I feel like most people do, right?\n\nIt’s hard to say because “mood swing” makes me think of someone going from totally fine to, like, yelling or crying out of nowhere, and I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, my family might have a different opinion—my mom always says I’m “a lot” when I’m home, but she also says that about my music and the way I leave my shoes everywhere, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active or fidgety in most situations, not just at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not really just class or work stuff. Most days I’m kind of moving from one thing to the next—class, clinicals, then I’ll go to the gym, run errands, whatever—and I don’t really leave empty space.\n\nLike if I have a gap in my schedule, I immediately try to put something in it. Even if it’s not important, I’ll be like, “Okay, I’ll go pick something up,” or “I should go do this thing,” because just sitting around feels… almost impossible. I’ll tell myself I’m going to relax, but then five minutes later I’m up doing something.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or behaving impulsively when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, apparently that was a thing when I was little. My parents still bring up these elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat or blurting stuff out.\n\nAnd I guess I’d rush ahead before they were even done giving directions, which… honestly still sounds like something I’d do. But yeah, that was way back in elementary school, not just something that started once I got into radiology training or college stuff.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more problems with impulsivity or restlessness than your friends when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember comparing myself to my friends at the time because when you’re a kid you’re just… doing kid stuff, you know? But my parents still bring up those elementary school conferences where teachers said I was always out of my seat, calling out, or rushing ahead before they’d even finished giving directions.\n\nSo I guess compared to other kids it must’ve stood out, since it kept coming up. And that was way before middle school or any of the radiology training stuff now.\n\nDoctor: Did your restlessness and impulsivity start when you were a child or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: It definitely started when I was a kid. My parents still bring up those elementary school conferences where teachers were saying I was always out of my seat, calling out, or rushing ahead before they were even done giving directions.\n\nI mean, I don’t remember every detail, but it wasn’t like it suddenly showed up with radiology training or college. It was happening way before middle school.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems in your personal relationships due to your restlessness or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, I can think of times where people got annoyed with me because I was kind of all over the place, but I don’t know if I’d call it “significant problems,” you know?\n\nWith friends it’s more like… I’ll interrupt or want to go do something right away, and then suddenly we’re talking about where to eat or whether someone can drive, and I’m already halfway planning the whole thing. But relationship-wise, like big fallout or anything, I don’t really know. I haven’t thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness or impulsivity in most areas of your life, such as at home, with friends, and in different activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s pretty much everywhere. Like most days I’m going from class to clinicals to the gym to errands, and I don’t really stop. Even if there’s a random gap, I immediately try to fill it with something, because just sitting around feels almost impossible.\n\nLike even at home, if I’m technically “free,” I’ll start thinking I should go do laundry or run to the store or… I don’t know, clean my car or something. It’s not even always important. I just feel like I have to be doing something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Even if there’s a random gap, I immediately try to fill it with something, because just sitting around feels almost impossible.\n\nLike even at home, if I’m technically “free,” I’ll start thinking I should go do laundry or run to the store or… I don’t know, clean my car or something. It’s not even always important. I just feel like I have to be doing something.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / radiologic_technologist_or_technician / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"My clinical evaluations have taken a hit, and I was put on a formal remediation plan because supervisors say my behavior disrupts patient flow and makes teamwork harder. I’m worried it could cost me my place in the program if I don’t get it under control.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I think it has. I was kind of trying not to make it sound dramatic, but my clinical evaluations have definitely taken a hit.

Like, in my radiology clinicals, supervisors have said my behavior can mess with the patient flow and make it harder for the team to work smoothly. And now I’m on a formal remediation plan, which feels awful, because it’s not just someone being annoyed with me anymore — it’s documented.

I keep thinking about it when I’m at home too, like I’ll be eating dinner or watching something and suddenly remember, “Oh right, this could actually affect whether I stay in the program.” So yeah, it’s causing problems. I’m honestly worried that if I don’t get it under control, it could cost me my spot.

Like if we’re just hanging out and it’s supposed to be relaxed, I’ll still start tapping on something or humming, or I’ll get up a bunch for no real reason.\n\nIt’s kind of the same as when I’m editing photos or sketching, which I actually like doing—I can’t keep it quiet for long. Like, I can get irritated or worked up sometimes, but I feel like most people do, right?\n\nIt’s hard to say because “mood swing” makes me think of someone going from totally fine to, like, yelling or crying out of nowhere, and I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, my family might have a different opinion—my mom always says I’m “a lot” when I’m home, but she also says that about my music and the way I leave my shoes everywhere, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active or fidgety in most situations, not just at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not really just class or work stuff. Most days I’m kind of moving from one thing to the next—class, clinicals, then I’ll go to the gym, run errands, whatever—and I don’t really leave empty space.\n\nLike if I have a gap in my schedule, I immediately try to put something in it. Even if it’s not important, I’ll be like, “Okay, I’ll go pick something up,” or “I should go do this thing,” because just sitting around feels… almost impossible. I’ll tell myself I’m going to relax, but then five minutes later I’m up doing something.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or behaving impulsively when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, apparently that was a thing when I was little. My parents still bring up these elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat or blurting stuff out.\n\nAnd I guess I’d rush ahead before they were even done giving directions, which… honestly still sounds like something I’d do. But yeah, that was way back in elementary school, not just something that started once I got into radiology training or college stuff.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more problems with impulsivity or restlessness than your friends when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember comparing myself to my friends at the time because when you’re a kid you’re just… doing kid stuff, you know? But my parents still bring up those elementary school conferences where teachers said I was always out of my seat, calling out, or rushing ahead before they’d even finished giving directions.\n\nSo I guess compared to other kids it must’ve stood out, since it kept coming up. And that was way before middle school or any of the radiology training stuff now.\n\nDoctor: Did your restlessness and impulsivity start when you were a child or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: It definitely started when I was a kid. My parents still bring up those elementary school conferences where teachers were saying I was always out of my seat, calling out, or rushing ahead before they were even done giving directions.\n\nI mean, I don’t remember every detail, but it wasn’t like it suddenly showed up with radiology training or college. It was happening way before middle school.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems in your personal relationships due to your restlessness or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. But relationship-wise, like big fallout or anything, I don’t really know. I haven’t thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness or impulsivity in most areas of your life, such as at home, with friends, and in different activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s pretty much everywhere. I just feel like I have to be doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it has. I’m honestly worried that if I don’t get it under control, it could cost me my spot.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder or ADHD?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no… I mean, not officially. I’ve never had someone sit me down and say, “you have that,” or anything like that.\n\nI guess I’ve wondered about it before, but then I’ll kind of talk myself out of it or get busy with school stuff and forget to actually follow up. Like I’ll suddenly decide I need to check something or grab something, even if I probably don’t. It’s just really hard to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or pay attention when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, when I’m sitting still I definitely notice the sitting still part more than anything—like my leg bouncing or needing to shift around. Focus is… I don’t know, it depends what’s going on. Sometimes I’m listening, but then I’ll get caught thinking about, like, whether I parked in the right place or if I need to message someone back, and then I realize I missed part of what was said. But I don’t know if that’s a focus thing or just me being uncomfortable sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I do. Like at work, if a supervisor is explaining how they want a positioning step done, I’ll jump in before they’re even finished—like I’m trying to show I know it, or answer the question right away. And then they’ll finish and I realize I missed the one part that actually mattered.\n\nIt’s kind of embarrassing, because I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve really… waited, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsivity causing problems in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly with supervisors and the team, I guess. Like, it’s not just “oh, he talks too much” or whatever—my clinical evaluations have actually gotten worse. They said I was disrupting patient flow and making teamwork harder, which… yeah, that was pretty hard to hear.\n\nI’m on a formal remediation plan now, and I keep thinking about it even when I’m not there—like, I’ll be driving home and then remember some random thing I said too fast, or how I cut in when someone was trying to coordinate the next patient. Sorry, I’m kind of jumping around. But yeah, it’s causing real problems. I’m honestly worried if I don’t get it under control, I could lose my spot in the program.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they've finished speaking in social situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I notice it way more in the program because there are consequences right away.\n\nLike, if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me something, I’ll jump in with the answer before they’re done talking, almost like I’m trying to get ahead of it. And then they finish and I realize I missed the part that actually mattered. So I know I do it there. Socially, I guess I’d have to think about it more—I’m not as aware of it unless someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or restless when you're doing things like watching TV or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like even watching TV, I’m shifting around or getting up for no real reason, but it’s worse when I’m supposed to be sitting still in the radiology suite or in class. That’s actually a big part of why I came in—I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin when I’m expected to just sit there.\n\nI’ll tell myself, okay, just tough it out, don’t make it obvious, but then I’m pacing, or changing positions, or finding some excuse to go do something. It’s been going on for months, and I keep thinking I should be able to just stop doing it, but I haven’t really been able to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying still is more bothersome to you than your difficulty paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the staying-still part is honestly what bothers me the most. Like, attention is a problem too, but the thing that made me come in was that feeling like I’m crawling out of my skin whenever I’m supposed to just sit there in the radiology suite or in class.\n\nAnd it’s not like I haven’t tried to just deal with it. For months I’ve been telling myself, okay, just sit still, don’t make it obvious, but then I’m shifting around, or pacing, or finding some excuse to get up and move. It’s just… it gets really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience restlessness or fidgeting in quiet activities outside of work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if it’s something I actually like, like editing photos or sketching, I still can’t keep it… quiet, I guess.\n\nI’ll start tapping my pencil or humming without realizing it, or I’ll keep getting up to grab something, then sit back down, then get up again. Sometimes I’ll start talking to whoever’s nearby, even if they’re trying to focus. Like in the lab, or at home, people will be like, “Can you settle down?” and I’m not even trying to be annoying, I just feel like I have to do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience restlessness or fidgeting when you're relaxing with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, with friends or family too. Like if we’re just hanging out and it’s supposed to be relaxed, I’ll still start tapping on something or humming, or I’ll get up a bunch for no real reason.\n\nIt’s kind of the same as when I’m editing photos or sketching, which I actually like doing—I can’t keep it quiet for long. I’ll start talking to whoever’s nearby, even if they’re doing their own thing, and then people at home are like, “Can you just settle down?” I don’t mean to be distracting, it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: Um… strong mood swings? I don’t know, I’m not sure I’d call them strong. Like, I can get irritated or worked up sometimes, but I feel like most people do, right?\n\nIt’s hard to say because “mood swing” makes me think of someone going from totally fine to, like, yelling or crying out of nowhere, and I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, my family might have a different opinion—my mom always says I’m “a lot” when I’m home, but she also says that about my music and the way I leave my shoes everywhere, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active or fidgety in most situations, not just at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not really just class or work stuff. Most days I’m kind of moving from one thing to the next—class, clinicals, then I’ll go to the gym, run errands, whatever—and I don’t really leave empty space.\n\nLike if I have a gap in my schedule, I immediately try to put something in it. Even if it’s not important, I’ll be like, “Okay, I’ll go pick something up,” or “I should go do this thing,” because just sitting around feels… almost impossible. I’ll tell myself I’m going to relax, but then five minutes later I’m up doing something.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or behaving impulsively when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, apparently that was a thing when I was little. My parents still bring up these elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat or blurting stuff out.\n\nAnd I guess I’d rush ahead before they were even done giving directions, which… honestly still sounds like something I’d do. But yeah, that was way back in elementary school, not just something that started once I got into radiology training or college stuff.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more problems with impulsivity or restlessness than your friends when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember comparing myself to my friends at the time because when you’re a kid you’re just… doing kid stuff, you know? But my parents still bring up those elementary school conferences where teachers said I was always out of my seat, calling out, or rushing ahead before they’d even finished giving directions.\n\nSo I guess compared to other kids it must’ve stood out, since it kept coming up. And that was way before middle school or any of the radiology training stuff now.\n\nDoctor: Did your restlessness and impulsivity start when you were a child or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: It definitely started when I was a kid. My parents still bring up those elementary school conferences where teachers were saying I was always out of my seat, calling out, or rushing ahead before they were even done giving directions.\n\nI mean, I don’t remember every detail, but it wasn’t like it suddenly showed up with radiology training or college. It was happening way before middle school.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems in your personal relationships due to your restlessness or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, I can think of times where people got annoyed with me because I was kind of all over the place, but I don’t know if I’d call it “significant problems,” you know?\n\nWith friends it’s more like… I’ll interrupt or want to go do something right away, and then suddenly we’re talking about where to eat or whether someone can drive, and I’m already halfway planning the whole thing. But relationship-wise, like big fallout or anything, I don’t really know. I haven’t thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness or impulsivity in most areas of your life, such as at home, with friends, and in different activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s pretty much everywhere. Like most days I’m going from class to clinicals to the gym to errands, and I don’t really stop. Even if there’s a random gap, I immediately try to fill it with something, because just sitting around feels almost impossible.\n\nLike even at home, if I’m technically “free,” I’ll start thinking I should go do laundry or run to the store or… I don’t know, clean my car or something. It’s not even always important. I just feel like I have to be doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it has. I was kind of trying not to make it sound dramatic, but my clinical evaluations have definitely taken a hit.\n\nLike, in my radiology clinicals, supervisors have said my behavior can mess with the patient flow and make it harder for the team to work smoothly. And now I’m on a formal remediation plan, which feels awful, because it’s not just someone being annoyed with me anymore — it’s documented.\n\nI keep thinking about it when I’m at home too, like I’ll be eating dinner or watching something and suddenly remember, “Oh right, this could actually affect whether I stay in the program.” So yeah, it’s causing problems. I’m honestly worried that if I don’t get it under control, it could cost me my spot.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder or ADHD?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no… I mean, not officially. I’ve never had someone sit me down and say, “you have that,” or anything like that.\n\nI guess I’ve wondered about it before, but then I’ll kind of talk myself out of it or get busy with school stuff and forget to actually follow up. So, yeah — no formal diagnosis that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and impulsivity are more prominent than problems with paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like, I do have moments where I’m not fully locked in, but the thing that feels the loudest is the restlessness.\n\nThat’s actually a big reason I came in — I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin when I’m supposed to just sit still in the radiology suite or in class. And I keep thinking I can just tough it out, like, “Okay, just sit here and be normal for this lecture,” but then I’m shifting around, standing up, pacing if I can, or finding some excuse to go do something.\n\nIt’s been months of that, and it’s not like I’m trying to be difficult. It just builds up until moving feels like the only way to make it stop for a second.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness and impulsivity consistently since you were a child, without any significant periods where these symptoms went away?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been there since I was little. My parents still bring up these elementary school conferences where the teachers would say I was always out of my seat or calling out answers, or I’d rush ahead before they even finished giving directions.\n\nAnd that was way before middle school — I mean, I remember the cafeteria being this whole loud chaos thing and I’d get distracted by who had the good snacks, but yeah, sorry — the main thing is, it wasn’t just something that started with radiology training. It’s kind of been a pattern for as long as people were paying attention to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
